<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{ass:rand-exp} $Y(\cdot) \noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\protect\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\mathpalette{\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\protect\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\noexpand\independenT}{\perp} Z \mid L$, the treatment assignment distribution $\mathbb{P}$ is known and $0 < \pi_i < 1$ for all $i\in[n]$.
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{ass:bounded}
    $Y_i(0),Y_i(1) \in [a,b]$ for all $i \in [n]$.
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:hoeffding}
    Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be independent random variables which have respective means $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_n$ and assume that $X_i \in [l_i,u_i]$ almost surely for all $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$. Let $\delta \in (0,1)$. Then, with probability $1-\delta$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left\vert \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i-\mu_i \right\vert \leq \sqrt{\tfrac{1}{n}{\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^n  (u_i-l_i)^2}}\,\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2n}}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:mp}
    Assume the setting of Theorem~\ref{thm:hoeffding} and denote the centred sample variance $\hat{V} = \frac{1}{n(n-1)} \sum_{i<j} \big((X_i-c_i) - (X_j-c_j)\big)^2$, where $c_i := (l_i+u_i)/2$. Define $L:=\max_{1\leq i \leq n}(u_i-l_i)$ and let $\delta \in (0,1)$. 
                      
                                                                            
                    
                                                                                                                                                                     
    Then, with probability $1-\delta$,
                      
                                                                                                                                               
                    
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left\vert \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i-\mu_i \right\vert \leq \sqrt{\frac{2 \hat{V} \log(3/\delta)}{n}} + \frac{7L \log(3/\delta)}{3(n-1)}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:mine-bernstein}
    Assume the setting of Theorem~\ref{thm:mp}, define $\epsilon_n := \frac{1}{n} \sum_{j=1}^{n} \frac{1}{j} =\mathcal{O}(\frac{\log(n)}{n})$ and let $\delta \in (0,1)$. Then, with probability $1-\delta$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left\vert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i - \mu_i\right\vert 
        &\leq (1+\epsilon_{n-1})\frac{\hat{V}}{L}\, h_1^{-1}\!\left(\frac{L^2\log(2/\delta)}{(1+\epsilon_{n-1})(n-1)\hat{V}}\right),\\
        &\leq\sqrt{\frac{2(1+\epsilon_{n-1})\,\hat{V} \,\log(2/\delta)}{n-1}} + \frac{L\log(2/\delta)}{n-1},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $h_1(u) := u - \log(1+u)$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:bernoulli-hoeffding}
    Suppose $Z\sim \mathrm{Bern}(\pi_1,\ldots,\pi_n)$, define $B:=\max\{\lvert a-m\rvert, \lvert b- m \rvert\}$ and let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Then, a $1-\alpha$ confidence interval for $\tau_n$ is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}_n^m \pm B \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{1}{\pi_i^2(1-\pi_i)^2}}\, \sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\alpha)}{2n}}\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Among the class of $m$-centred estimators, $\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{mid}}_n$ has the shortest Hoeffding confidence interval, which is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{mid}}_n \pm \frac{b-a}{2} \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{1}{\pi_i^2(1-\pi_i)^2}}\, \sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\alpha)}{2n}}\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The $m$-centred estimator is the average of the independent random variables $\hat{\tau}^m_{i,n}$
                  
                                                                                             
                
which can assume two values each. Hence, the length of the support of $\hat{\tau}^m_{i,n}$ is given by
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    u_i-l_i
        = \left\vert\frac{Y_i(1)-m}{\pi_i}+\frac{Y_i(0)-m}{1-\pi_i} \right\vert
        \leq \frac{\max\{\lvert a -m \rvert, \lvert b-m\rvert\}}{\pi_i(1-\pi_i)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
                                                                                                                                   
                   
                                                                                                                                                   
                                                                                                                                              
                 
                                                                                
                      
                                                                                                          
                    
                                                                                          
                   
                                                                                                                                     
                                                                                 
                                                                                  
                 
    Applying Theorem~\ref{thm:hoeffding} yields the first statement of the theorem. Lastly, we see that the choice $m=\frac{a+b}{2}$ minimizes the constant $B$ and thus the length of the confidence interval.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:bern-sandoval}
    Suppose $Z\sim \mathrm{Bern}(\pi_1,\ldots,\pi_n)$ and the treatment assignment probabilities are equal, i.e.\ $\pi:=\pi_1=\ldots=\pi_n$. Let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Then, a $1-\alpha$ confidence interval for $\tau_n$ is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}_n^m \pm  \frac{\log(2/\alpha)+n\gamma}{n\lambda}\right],
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $\gamma$ and $\lambda$ are defined as
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        {\begin{gather*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \gamma := \log\left(\frac{u}{u-l}e^{\lambda l} - \frac{l}{u-l}e^{\lambda u}\right),\qquad
        \lambda := \sqrt{\frac{2\log(2/\alpha)}{n(-l)u}},
                                                                 
                                                                
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        {\end{gather*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    with $l:=\min\{(a-m)/\pi, (m-b)/(1-\pi)\}$ and $u:= \max\{(b-m)/\pi,(m-a)/(1-\pi)\}$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
In the proof of their theorem~3.5, \citet{sandoval_nonasymptotic_2026} consider the difference of an ($m$-centred) estimator and the SATE:
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \hat{\tau}_n^m-\tau_n &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \left(\frac{Z_i}{\pi_i}-1\right)(Y_i(1)-m) - \left(\frac{1-Z_i}{1-\pi_i}-1\right)(Y_i(0)-m)\\
        &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{(1-\pi_i)Y_i(1)+\pi_i Y_i(0) -m}{\pi_i(1-\pi_i)} (Z_i-\pi_i) =: \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n X_i.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
                      
                                                                                                                                                        
                    
    We see that the random variables $X_i$ are upper- and lower-bounded as follows
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \min\left\{\frac{a-m}{\pi},\frac{m-b}{1-\pi}\right\}\leq X_i \leq \max\left\{\frac{b-m}{\pi},\frac{m-a}{1-\pi}\right\}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    We define $l$ as the minimum and $u$ as the maximum.\footnote{\citeauthor{sandoval_nonasymptotic_2026} use the notation $a$ and $b$ instead.} The result now follows from the same steps in their proof with the improved bounds on the $X_i$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:confint-bernstein-indep}
    Suppose $Z\sim \mathrm{Bern}(\pi_1,\ldots,\pi_n)$, $n\geq 2$ and let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Define $\underline{\pi} := \min_{1 \leq i \leq n} \{\pi_i, 1-\pi_i\}$, $\hat{V} := \frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^n (\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{mid}}_{i,n}-\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{mid}})^2$ and $\epsilon_n := \frac{1}{n} \sum_{j=1}^{n} \frac{1}{j}$. Then, an explicit $1-\alpha$ confidence interval for~$\tau_n$ is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        {\begin{gather*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{mid}}_n \pm \left(\sqrt{\frac{2(1+\epsilon_{n-1})\hat{V}\log(2/\alpha)}{n-1}} + \frac{b-a}{\underline{\pi}}\frac{\log(2/\alpha)}{n-1}\right)\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@gather*} 
        {\end{gather*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@gather*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    The tighter implicit $1-\alpha$ confidence interval takes the form
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{mid}}_n \pm (1+\epsilon_{n-1})\frac{\underline{\pi}}{b-a} \hat{V}\, h_1^{-1}\!\left(\frac{(b-a)^2\log(2/\alpha)}{\underline{\pi}^2(1+\epsilon_{n-1})(n-1)\hat{V}}\right)\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
Suppose $Z \sim \mathrm{MP}(n)$, $n\geq 4$ and let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Then, a $1-\alpha$ Hoeffding confidence interval for $\tau_n$ is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}_n \pm 2 (b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\alpha)}{n}}\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Let $\hat{V}^*:= \frac{1}{n/2-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2} (\hat{\tau}^*_{i,n}-\hat{\tau}_n)^2$ and $\epsilon_n := \frac{1}{n} \sum_{j=1}^{n} \frac{1}{j}$. An explicit $1-\alpha$ Bernstein-type confidence interval is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}_n \pm \left(\sqrt{\frac{2(1+\epsilon_{n/2-1})\hat{V}^*\log(2/\alpha)}{n/2-1}} + 2(b-a)\frac{\log(2/\alpha)}{n/2-1}\right)\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}}

    }

    }

<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:cre-hoeffding}
    Suppose $Z\sim \mathrm{CRE}(n_0,n_1)$, define $\epsilon'_n := \frac{\epsilon_n-1}{n-\epsilon_n} = \mathcal{O}(\frac{\log(n)}{n})$, where $\epsilon_n = \sum_{j=1}^n \frac{1}{j}$ and let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Then, a $1-\alpha$ confidence interval for $\tau_n$ is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left[\hat{\tau}_n \pm \frac{b-a}{2} \frac{1}{\pi(1-\pi)}\, \sqrt{\frac{(1+\epsilon_n')\log(2/\alpha)}{2n}}\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Like in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:bern-sandoval}, we can express the difference of an $m$-centred estimator and the SATE as follows
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \hat{\tau}_n^m-\tau_n = \sum_{i=1}^n \left(\frac{1}{n}\frac{(1-\pi)Y_i(1)+\pi Y_i(0) -m}{\pi(1-\pi)}\right) (Z_i-\pi) =: \sum_{i=1}^n w_i X_i.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    All $m$-centred estimators are equal to the HT estimator $\hat{\tau}$, but inserting the additional hyper-parameter helps us to tighten the constant of the interval and make it scale-invariant. Since $X_i \in \{-\pi,1-\pi\}$, the $X_i$ are supported on an interval of length $1$; moreover, $\bar{X}=0$. The norm of the weights can be upper bounded like
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \lVert w \rVert_2^2 \leq \frac{1}{n^2} \frac{1}{\pi^2(1-\pi)^2} n B^2,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $B=\max\{\lvert a-m\rvert, \lvert b- m \rvert\}$. Choosing $B = (a+b)/2$ minimizes the upper bound and we obtain, $\lVert w \rVert_2 \leq \frac{b-a}{2}\frac{1}{\pi(1-\pi)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}$. The confidence interval now directly follows from applying Theorem~\ref{prop:rina-hoeffding} with $N=n$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:cre-sandoval}
    Suppose $Z\sim \mathrm{CRE}(n_0,n_1)$ and $\pi = 1/K$ for an integer $K \geq 2$. Let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Then, a $1-\alpha$ confidence interval for $\tau_n$ is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:cre-sandoval}
        \left[\hat{\tau}_n \pm \frac{b-a}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2/\alpha)}{n}}\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:neyman-conc}
 Suppose $Z \sim \mathrm{CRE}(n_0, n_1)$, where $n_0,n_1 \geq 2$, and let $\delta \in (0,1)$. Then, with probability $1-\delta$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    V^{1/2} \leq \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}]^{1/2} \leq \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^{1/2} + (b-a) \sqrt{\frac{c(n_0,n_1)\log(1/\delta)}{n}},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the constant is given by
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    c(n_0,n_1) := \frac{n\,(n_0^2+n_1^2)^2}{n_0n_1^3(n_0-1) + n_1 n_0^3(n_1-1)}.                              
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    }

        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
First, in Lemma~\ref{lem:self-bounded}, we prove that Neyman's variance estimator has a self-boundedness property with respect to the swap operator, which permutes the treatment assignments of two units.
    
    Second, we realize that the completely randomized assignment mechanism is the stationary distribution of the Markov chain pertaining to the $n_1$-particle Bernoulli-Laplace diffusion model on $n$ sites. For these processes modified log-Sobolev concentration inequalities have been studied in the literature \citep{lee_logarithmic_1998} and also been recently linked to sampling without replacement \citep{sambale_concentration_2022}. In Theorem~\ref{thm:mod-log-sob}, we re-state the sharp inequality of \citet{salez_sharp_2021} in our setting.
    
    Third, we combine this concentration inequality with the self-boundedness property of~$\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}$, and use an adapted version of Herbst's argument \citep{boucheron_concentration_2013}[chap.\ 6] to complete the proof.
<!-- end proof-4 -->

<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Instead of basing our proof on \citet{barber_hoeffding_2024}'s Bernstein inequality, we could have also used \citet{bardenet_concentration_2015}'s concentration inequality for sampling without replacement. This generally leads to longer intervals due to the larger constant in the $\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})$-term, however. For the interested reader, we provide the corresponding proposition and a comparison with the confidence intervals of Proposition~\ref{prop:cre-bernstein} in Appendix~\ref{app:cre-bernstein-bardenet}.

\citet{greene_exponential_2017} also prove a Bernstein inequality for sampling from a finite population without replacement. Yet, their result uses the variance of a majorizing population which may be considerably larger than the actual variance. For this reason, we did not pursue a confidence interval based on their concentration inequality in this article.
<!-- end remark-1 -->

\label{prop:rina-hoeffding}
    Let $w \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $X_1,\ldots,X_N \in [l,u]$ be exchangeable random variables, where $N \geq n \geq 2$. Define $\bar{X} := \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^N X_i$ and $\epsilon'_N := \frac{\epsilon_N-1}{N-\epsilon_N} = \mathcal{O}(\frac{\log(N)}{N})$, where $\epsilon_N = \sum_{j=1}^N \frac{1}{j}$. Then, for any $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$,

<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The Hoeffding bound is based on Proposition~A.1 in~\citet{barber_hoeffding_2024}. Tracing the more general, original proof of this result in~\citet{waudby-smith_confidence_2020}, we find that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        M_t = \prod_{i=1}^t \exp\left(\lambda v_i (X_i-\bar{X}_{\geq i})-\frac{\lambda^2 (u-l)^2 v_i^2 }{8}\right)
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    is a super-martingale for any $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$. Now, the proposition immediately follows from replacing the factor $\frac{\lambda^2}{2}$ in the proof of~\citet{barber_hoeffding_2024}[Thm.~3.1] with $\frac{\lambda^2(u-l)^2}{8}$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:rina-bernstein}
    Let $w \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $X_1,\ldots,X_n \in [l,u]$ be exchangeable random variables, where $N \geq n \geq 2$. Define $\bar{X}$ and $\epsilon_N'$ as in Theorem~\ref{prop:rina-hoeffding} and set $\sigma_X^2:= \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^N(X_i-\bar{X})^2$. Then, for any $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$ such that $\lvert \lambda \rvert < 3/((u-l)\lVert w \rVert_\infty (1+\epsilon_N'))$,

        \left\vert\sum_{i=1}^n w_i (X_i - \bar{X})\right\vert \leq \frac{\tilde{\sigma}^2_{X,N} \lVert w \rVert_2^2}{(u-l)\lVert w \rVert_\infty}\, h_2^{-1}\left(\frac{(1+\epsilon_N')(u-l)^2\lVert w\rVert^2_\infty\log(2/\delta)}{\tilde{\sigma}^2_{X,N}\lVert w \rVert_2^2}\right),

    where $h_2(u) := (1+u)\log(1+u)-u$.

<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The first, explicit concentration inequality follows from a minor modification of the original proof. Since $X_i \in [l,u]$ for all $i \in \{1,\ldots,N\}$, we have $\lvert v_i (X_i-\bar{X}_{\geq i})\rvert \leq (u-l) \lVert v \rVert_\infty$ for any $v \in \mathbb{R}^N$. Now, the result directly follows from replacing the factor $\frac{2}{3}$ with $\frac{u-l}{3}$ in the proofs of \cite{barber_hoeffding_2024}[Prop.~B.1., Thm.~3.3].

    For the second, implicit concentration inequality we use \citet{bennett_1962}'s inequality in place of a Bernstein bound in the proofs of \cite{barber_hoeffding_2024}. First, we consider their Proposition~B.1. Replacing the Bernstein with a Bennett inequality \citep{boucheron_concentration_2013}[Thm.~2.9], we can show that the process

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        M_t = \prod_{i=1}^t \exp\left(\lambda v_i(X_i - \bar{X}_{\geq i}) - \frac{v_i^2\sigma^2_{X_{\geq i}}}{(u-l)^2\lVert v\rVert_\infty^2}\phi\Big(\lambda (u-l)\lVert v\rVert_\infty\Big)\right)

Next, we follow the same steps as in the proof of \citeauthor{barber_hoeffding_2024}'s Theorem~3.3 with the factor $\phi(\lambda (u-l)\lVert v\rVert_\infty)/((u-l)^2\lVert v\rVert_\infty^2)$ instead of $\lambda^2/(2(1-\frac{2\lvert\lambda\rvert}{3}\lVert v \rVert_\infty))$. <!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:bm}
    Let $\mathcal{X}:=(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ be a finite collection of real-valued numbers. Assume that they have mean $0$, are contained in the interval $[-B,B]$, where $B>0$, and denote their variance $\sigma^2 = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2$. Further, let $X_1,\ldots,X_k$ be a random sample from $\mathcal{X}$ without replacement, where $k\leq n/2$ and let $\delta \in (0,1)$. Then, with probability $1-3\delta$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left\vert\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^k X_i\right\vert \leq \sigma \sqrt{\frac{n-k+1}{n\,k}2\log(1/\delta)}+\left(\frac{4}{3k} + \sqrt{\frac{k-1}{n\,k\,(n-k+1)}}\right) B\log(1/\delta).

        \left\vert\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^k X_i\right\vert \leq \frac{v}{2B}\, h^{-1}_2\!\!\left(\frac{4B^2}{v}\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{k}\right),

We prove the two results separately.
    
    \textit{Bernstein Bound} The first statement follows from Corollary~3.6 in \cite{bardenet_concentration_2015} with a few minor modifications. Their $n$ and $N$ correspond to our $k$ and $n$; moreover, we only consider the case $k\leq n/2$. Lastly, our random variables are centred ($\mu=0$) which allows us to replace the factor $b-a$ with $B$; see also the proofs of their Proposition~3.2 and Lemma~3.3.
                                                                                                                                                                      

    \textit{Bennett Bound} This result can be proven using the derivations in the proof of \cite[Thm.~3.5]{bardenet_concentration_2015}. To help the reader understand the structure of the argument, we provide the main steps here and refer to \cite{bardenet_concentration_2015} for the details. Note that we adapt the notation to our setting and replace $b-a$ with $B$ as explained above. References in the following paragraph invoke results from \cite{bardenet_concentration_2015} and not this article.

    Define $\varphi(x) := (e^x-1-x)/x^2 $ and let $\lambda >0$. Combining Proposition~3.2 and equation~(17) in Lemma~3.3, we obtain that with probability $1-2\delta$
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \max_{1\leq j \leq k}\, \frac{1}{j}\sum_{i=1}^j X_j &\leq \frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\lambda} + \frac{\lambda}{k^2}\varphi\left(\frac{2B\lambda}{k}\right) \left[\sigma^2+\frac{\sigma B(k-1)}{n-k+1}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{k-1}}\right]\sum_{j=1}^k \frac{k^2}{(n-j)^2}\\
        &\leq \frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\lambda} + \frac{\lambda}{k^2}\varphi\left(\frac{2B\lambda}{k}\right) (k+1) \gamma^2.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    The second inequality follows from the estimation in~(10) and we define

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \gamma^2 := \left[\sigma^2+\frac{\sigma B(k-1)}{n-k+1}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{k-1}}\right] \frac{1}{k+1} \frac{k^3}{(n-k)^2}\left(1-\frac{k-1}{n}\right).

    We can invert this statement above and get

        \mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j \leq k}\, \frac{1}{j}\sum_{i=1}^j X_j \geq t\right)\leq \exp\left(-\lambda t +\frac{\lambda^2(k+1)}{k^2}\varphi\left(\frac{2B\lambda}{k}\right) \gamma^2\right) +\delta.

    for all $t>0$. We can now follow the same steps as in \citeauthor{bardenet_concentration_2015}'s article to find the optimal $\lambda$, where $\gamma^2$ plays the role of $\tilde{\gamma}^2$. Plugging this optimal choice of $\lambda$ into the inequality above yields

        \mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j \leq k}\, \frac{1}{j}\sum_{i=1}^j X_j \geq t\right) \leq \exp\left(-\frac{k}{2Bu}h(ut)\right)+\delta,

    where

        u := \frac{2B(n-k)^2/k^2}{\frac{n-k+1}{n}\sigma^2+\frac{\sigma B(k-1)}{n}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{k-1}}}.
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

   Solving the right-hand side for $t$, we obtain

        t = \frac{1}{u}\, h^{-1}_2\!\!\left(\frac{2Bu\log(1/\delta)}{k}\right).

    Hence, with probability $1-2\delta$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

        \frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^k X_i \leq \frac{1}{u}\, h^{-1}_2\!\!\left(\frac{2Bu\log(1/\delta)}{k}\right)

    Lastly, we can repeat the same derivations for the random variables $-X_j$ to get a lower bound and combine the two via a union bound. Note that we need to account for the upper bound on the variance only once. We state the final result in terms of $v := \frac{2B}{u}$.

<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We initially prove the concentration inequality for the case $[l_i,u_i] = [0,1]$ for all $i \in \{1,\ldots,n\}$. At the end of the proof, we show how the result in this ``base case'' can be directly extended to the general setting by a linear transformation of the random variables.

    Define the function $\psi(\lambda) := -\log(1-\lambda)-\lambda$, let $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n \in (0,1)$ be deterministic numbers and define $\bar{X}_{<i} := \frac{1}{i-1}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1} X_j$ for $i \geq 2$ and $\bar{X}_{<1}:=0$. First, we show that the process
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        M_t := \prod_{i=1}^t \exp \left(\lambda_i(X_i-\mu_i)-\psi(\lambda_i)(X_i-\bar{X}_{<i})^2\right)
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    is a super-martingale with respect to the filtration $\mathcal{F}_t:=\sigma(X_1,\ldots,X_t)$. A very similar result was shown by \citet[Thm.~4]{howard_time-uniform_2021}: They set all $\lambda_i$ to be equal and use a general predictable sequence $\hat{X}_i$ where we use $\bar{X}_{<i}$.

    It is easy to see that $(M_t)_t$ is adapted to the filtration and the integrability condition is fulfilled as the $X_i$ are bounded. Hence, it remains to show that $\mathbb{E}[M_{t}\mid \mathcal{F}_{t-1}] \leq M_{t-1}$ almost surely for all $t\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$. First, we use the product structure of $(M_t)_t$ and measurability to re-write the expression:
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

Suppose $Z\sim \mathrm{Bern}(\pi_1,\ldots,\pi_n)$ and let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Define $\underline{\pi} := \min_{1 \leq i \leq n} \{\pi_i, 1-\pi_i\}$,

        L(\underline{\pi}) := \max\left\{\frac{\max\{\lvert a-m\rvert, \lvert b -m \rvert\}}{\underline{\pi}(1-\underline{\pi})}, \frac{b-a}{\underline{\pi}}\right\},

    and $\epsilon_n := \frac{1}{n} \sum_{j=1}^{n} \frac{1}{j}$. Moreover, set $\hat{V} := \frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i<j} ((\hat{\tau}^m_{i,n}-c_i)-(\hat{\tau}^m_{j,n}-c_j))^2$, where
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        c_i := \left(\max\left\{\frac{b-m}{\pi_i},\frac{m-a}{1-\pi_i}\right\}+\min\left\{\frac{a-m}{\pi_i},\frac{m-b}{1-\pi_i}\right\}\right)\Big/\,2.

        \hat{\tau}^m_{n,i} \in [l_i, u_i] := \left[\min\left\{\frac{a-m}{\pi_i}, \frac{m-b}{1-\pi_i}\right\}, \max\left\{\frac{b-m}{\pi_i}, \frac{m-a}{1-\pi_i}\right\}\right].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

    Its midpoint is given by $c_i$ and, setting $B:=\max\{\lvert a-m\rvert, \lvert b -m \rvert\}$, we compute an upper bound for its length:
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        u_i-l_i &= \max\left\{\frac{b-m}{\pi_i}, \frac{m-a}{1-\pi_i}\right\} + \max\left\{\frac{m-a}{\pi_i}, \frac{b-m}{1-\pi_i}\right\}\\
        &= \max\left\{\frac{b-m}{\pi_i(1-\pi_i)}, \frac{m-a}{\pi_i(1-\pi_i)}, \frac{b-a}{\pi_i}, \frac{b-a}{1-\pi_i}\right\}\\
        &\leq \max\left\{\frac{B}{\underline{\pi}(1-\underline{\pi})}, \frac{b-a}{\underline{\pi}}\right\}=:L(\underline{\pi})

    For the midpoint-differenced estimator, $B$ equals $(b-a)/2$ and $L(\underline{\pi})$ simplifies accordingly:

        L(\underline{\pi}) = \frac{b-a}{\underline{\pi}} \max\left\{\frac{1}{2(1-\underline{\pi})},1\right\} = \frac{b-a}{\underline{\pi}},

<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
Comparing to the derivation of the Hoeffding inequality, we notice that the upper bound on $u_i-l_i$ is tighter than in the proof above, cf.\ proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:bernoulli-hoeffding}. This is a remnant of the fact that we additionally need to compute the midpoint of the support for the variance estimator $\hat{V}$ for Bernstein-type inequalities. This is why we choose a potentially larger interval $[u_i, l_i]$ whose endpoints are fully known.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:self-bounded}
	Define the swap operator $\tau_{ij}\colon \{0,1\}^n \to \{0,1\}^n$ as
	                  
    		$\tau_{ij}(z) = (z_1,\ldots, z_{i-1},z_j,z_{i+1},\ldots, z_{j-1}, z_i, z_{j+1},\ldots,z_n)$,
    	                
	where $i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$. Neyman's variance estimator is self-bounded in the sense that
	 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
		\sum_{i,j=1}^n \big(\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)\big)_+^2\, \leq\, 2 (b-a)^2\,c(n_0,n_1)\, \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z),
	 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
	where the constant is given by
	 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
		c(n_0,n_1) := \frac{n\,(n_0^2+n_1^2)^2}{n_0n_1^3(n_0-1) + n_1 n_0^3(n_1-1)}.                              
	 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}

<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We abbreviate the left-hand side of the inequality as $L(Z) := \sum_{i,j=1}^n \big(\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)\big)_+^2$. First, we notice that only index pairs $(i,j)$ with $Z \neq \tau_{ij} Z$ contribute to the sum; hence,
	 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
		L(Z) &= \sum_{i,j=1}^n \big(\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)\big)_+^2 \mathbf{1} \{Z \neq \tau_{ij} Z\}\\
		&= \sum_{i,j=1}^n \big(\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)\big)_+^2 \Big(Z_i (1-Z_j) + (1-Z_i)Z_j\Big)\\
		&= 2\sum_{i,j=1}^n Z_i (1-Z_j) \big(\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)\big)_+^2,
	 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
	where the last step follows from the symmetry in the summation. Next, we separate the Neyman variance estimator into its two summands which are given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}} = \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^1+\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^0 := \frac{n}{n_1(n_1-1)}\sum_{i \in I_1} (Y_i(1)-\hat{\bar{Y}}(1))^2 + \frac{n}{n_0(n_0-1)}\sum_{i \in I_0} (Y_i(0)-\hat{\bar{Y}}(0))^2,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $I_k := \{i\colon Z_i=k\}$ for $k\in \{0,1\}$. We fix indices $i\in I_1$ and $j \in I_0$ and consider the first summand under $Z$ and the swapped treatment $\tau_{ij}Z$:
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:mod-log-sob}
Define the entropy of a non-negative random variable $X$ as $\operatorname{Ent}[X] := \mathbb{E}[X\log(X)]-\mathbb{E}[X]\log(\mathbb{E}[X])$, let $g \colon \{0,1\}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ be a positive function and $Z \sim \mathrm{CRE}(n_0,n_1)$. Then, $g$ fulfills the modified log-Sobolev inequality
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \operatorname{Ent}[g(Z)] \leq\, \frac{1}{2n}\,  \mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{1\leq i < j \leq n} (g(Z)-g(\tau_{ij}Z))(\log g(Z)-\log g(\tau_{ij}Z))\right].
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
The completely randomized treatment assignment random vector is uniformly distributed over the 2-multislice. \cite{salez_sharp_2021}[Lem.~1.3] proved that regardless of $(n_0,n_1)$ the constant in the modified log-Sobolev inequality is always upper bounded by $1$ which yields the result.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Let $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$. We invoke Theorem~\ref{thm:mod-log-sob} with the function $g = e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}}$ and obtain a modified log-Sobolev inequality for the Neyman variance estimator. We modify its right-hand side so that the summation extends over all index pairs $(i,j)$ and so that it involves the positive part in view of Lemma~\ref{lem:self-bounded}:

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \operatorname{Ent}[e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)}] &\leq \frac{1}{2n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n} (e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)}-e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)})\,(\lambda\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\lambda\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z))\right]\\
        &= \frac{1}{4n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i,j=1}^n (e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)}-e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)})\,(\lambda\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\lambda\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z))\right]\\
        &=

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [t]

            &-\mathbb{E}\left[(e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)}-e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z)})\,(\lambda\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\lambda\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z))_-\right]

        &\leq \frac{\lambda^2}{2n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i,j=1}^n e^{\lambda \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)}(\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(Z)-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}(\tau_{ij}Z))_+^2\right]
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    The first equality stems from the symmetry of the swap operator, i.e.\ $\tau_{ij} = \tau_{ji}$, and that the ``diagonal swaps'' do not contribute, i.e.\ $\tau_{ii}Z=Z$. We obtain the positive part since $Z$ is uniformly distributed over the 2-multislice and thus $\tau_{ij} Z$ and $Z$ have the same distribution. (In the literature on concentration inequalities for random walks, a similar argument is used when the generator of the random walk satisfies a detailed-balance condition, see \cite{adamczak_concentration_2021} for instance.) The last inequality follows from $(e^x-e^y)(x-y)_+ \leq e^x(x-y)_+^2$.

    Invoking Lemma~\ref{lem:self-bounded}, we find the following bound on the entropy

Since we are interested in the left tail, however, we consider the log-mgf of $\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}]-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}$ denoted $\tilde{G}(\lambda)$.         &&\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}] &\leq \hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}} + \frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\lambda} + \frac{\lambda c }{1+c\lambda}\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}],\\

As we have seen in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cre-hoeffding}, we can express the difference of the HT estimator and the SATE as follows
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \hat{\tau}_n-\tau = \hat{\tau}_n^m-\tau = \sum_{i=1}^n\frac{1}{n} \left(\frac{Y_i(1)-\bar{Y}(1)}{\pi}+\frac{Y_i(0)-\bar{Y}(0)}{1-\pi}\right)(Z_i-\pi)=:\sum_{i=1}^n w_i X_i.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Here, we set $m=(1-\pi)\bar{Y}(1)+\pi \bar{Y}(0)$,
where $\bar{Y}(k):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n Y(k)$ for $k\in \{0,1\}$. Since $X_i \in \{-\pi,1-\pi\}$, the exchangeable random variables $X_i$ are supported on an interval of length~$1$. Furthermore, $\bar{X} = 0$ and the empirical variance of the $X_i$ is given by
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \sigma_X^2 = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (Z_i-\pi)^2 = \frac{1}{n} \left(n_1 \frac{n_0^2}{n^2}+n_0\frac{n_1^2}{n^2}\right) = \frac{n_0\,n_1}{n^2}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

Since the weights $w_i$ depend on unobserved potential outcomes, we need to find known or estimable upper bounds on their $L^\infty$- and $L^2$-norms to apply Theorem~\ref{thm:rina-bernstein}. First, the maximum value of the~$w_i$ can be estimated as follows
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \lVert w \rVert_\infty \leq \frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}+\frac{1}{1-\pi}\right) \frac{n-1}{n} (b-a) = \frac{n-1}{n_0\,n_1}(b-a).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Next, we re-write the squared $L^2$-norm of the weights:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \lVert w \rVert_2^2 &= \frac{1}{n^2} \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{(Y_i(1)-\bar{Y}(1))^2}{\pi^2} + \frac{(Y_i(0)-\bar{Y}(0))^2}{(1-\pi)^2} + \frac{2(Y_i(1)-\bar{Y}(1))(Y_i(0)-\bar{Y}(0))}{\pi(1-\pi)}\\
    &= \frac{n-1}{n_1^2}S^2(1)+\frac{n-1}{n_0^2}S^2(0)+\frac{2(n-1)}{n_0\,n_1}S^2(1,0).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since we cannot consistently estimate this quantity due to the cross-term $S^2(1,0)$, we use an upper bound, see \cite[Lem.~4.1]{ding_first_2024} for instance:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \lVert w \rVert_2^2 \leq S^2(1)\left(\frac{n-1}{n_1^2}+\frac{n-1}{n_0\,n_1}\right) + S^2(0)\left(\frac{n-1}{n_0^2}+\frac{n-1}{n_0\,n_1}\right) = \frac{n-1}{n_0\,n_1} \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}].
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
We can now derive the explicit Bernstein and the implicit Bennett confidence interval.

    \textit{Bernstein Interval}
    We apply Theorem~\ref{thm:rina-bernstein} with $N=n$ and the bounds on the respective expressions above and obtain that with probability $1-2\delta$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        {

\label{prop:cre-bernstein-bm}
    Suppose $Z \sim \mathrm{CRE}(n_0,n_1)$, where $n_0 \geq n_1 \geq 2$, and let $\alpha \in (0,1)$. Let $c(n_0,n_1)$ be defined as in Theorem~\ref{thm:neyman-conc} and set $\epsilon_n''' := \frac{1}{n_0}-\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{nn_0}$. Then, an explicit Bernstein $1-\alpha$ confidence interval for $\tau_n$ is given by

    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

To find empirical concentration inequalities for the HT estimator that account for the variance, we use modified versions of the results that \cite{bardenet_concentration_2015} obtained for sampling without replacement. To this end, we manipulate $\hat{\tau} - \tau$ as follows
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \hat{\tau}_n - \tau_n &= \frac{1}{n_1} \sum_{i=1}^n Z_i Y_i(1) - \frac{1}{n_0} \sum_{i=1}^n (1-Z_i) Y_i(0) - \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n Y_i(1)-Y_i(0) \\
        &= \frac{1}{n_1} \sum_{i=1}^n \left(\underbrace{(Y_i(1)-\bar{Y}(1))+\frac{n_1}{n_0}(Y_i(0)-\bar{Y}(0))}_{=:x_i}\right) Z_i,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $\bar{Y}(k) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n Y_i(k)$. This shows, that $\hat{\tau}-\tau$ can also be regarded as the average of~$n_1$ data points sampled without replacement from the population $(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$. Due to the definition of the $x_i$, their mean equals 0 -- $\mu = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i =0$ -- and
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \lVert x \rVert_\infty \leq \frac{n-1}{n} (b-a) + \frac{n_1}{n_0}\frac{n-1}{n} (b-a) = \frac{n-1}{n_0} (b-a).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Moreover, the (finite population) variance is given by
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \sigma^2 &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2\\
        &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n (Y_i(1)-\bar{Y}(1))^2+\frac{n_1^2}{n_0^2} (Y_i(0)-\bar{Y}(0))^2 + 2\frac{n_1}{n_0}(Y_i(1)-\bar{Y}(1))(Y_i(0)-\bar{Y}(0))\\
        &= \frac{n-1}{n} S^2(1) + \frac{n_1^2}{n_0^2} \frac{n-1}{n}S^2(0) + 2\frac{n_1}{n_0}\frac{n-1}{n} S^2(1,0).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Since $\sigma^2$ cannot be consistently estimated due to the cross-term $S^2(1,0)$, we use an upper bound, see \cite[Lem.~4.1]{ding_first_2024} for instance:
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:sigma-upper-bound}
        \sigma^2                                                                                                        
        \leq \frac{n-1}{n} \left(\frac{n}{n_0}S^2(1)+\frac{n \,n_1}{n_0^2}S^2(0)\right) = \frac{n-1}{n}\frac{n_1}{n_0} \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}].
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
                                                                                                  
    We are now in position to derive the explicit and implicit confidence intervals.


    \textit{Bernstein Interval} We apply Theorem~\ref{thm:bm} with $k=n_1$, $B=\frac{n-1}{n_0}(b-a)$ and obtain, that with probability $1-3\delta$
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \left\vert\frac{1}{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i Z_i\right\vert \leq \sigma \sqrt{\frac{n_0+1}{n\,n_1}2\log(1/\delta)}+\left(\frac{4}{3n_1} + \sqrt{\frac{n_1-1}{n\,n_1\,(n_0+1)}}\right) \frac{n-1}{n_0}(b-a)\log(1/\delta).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Moreover, due to Theorem~\ref{thm:neyman-conc}, with probability $1-\delta$:
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \sigma \leq \sqrt{\frac{(n-1)n_1}{n\,n_0}}\, \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}]^{1/2} \leq \sqrt{\frac{(n-1)n_1}{n\,n_0}}\, \left(\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^{1/2} +(b-a) \sqrt{\frac{c(n_0,n_1)\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right).

Throughout this proof, we tacitly assume $j\in \{1,\ldots,J\}$.

    \textit{Hoeffding Interval} In the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cre-hoeffding}, we have shown that

    }

        \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda_j (\hat{\tau}_{n_j}^j - \mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}_{n_j}^j])\right)\right] \leq
        \exp\left(\frac{\lambda_j^2}{8}\left(\frac{b-a}{2}\right)^2 \frac{n_j^3}{n_{j,0}^2\,n_{j,1}^2} (1+\epsilon_{n_j}')\right),
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    for all $j$ and $\lambda_j \in \mathbb{R}$. Let now $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$ and set $\lambda_j = \lambda s_j$. Due to independence of the $\hat{\tau}_{n_j}^j$, we obtain
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}}

        \mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\left(\lambda (\hat{\tau}_n-\tau_n)\right)\Big] &=
        \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda \sum_{j=1}^J s_j (\hat{\tau}_{n_j}^j - \tau_n)\right)\right] \\
        &\leq \exp\left(
        \frac{\lambda^2}{8} \left(\frac{b-a}{2}\right)^2 \sum_{j=1}^J s_j^2 \frac{n_j^3}{n_{j,0}^2\,n_{j,1}^2} (1+\epsilon_{n_j}')\right).
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    Inverting this bound on the moment generating function analogously to Theorem~\ref{prop:rina-hoeffding}, we see that with probability $1-\alpha$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        \left\vert \hat{\tau}_n-\tau_n \right\vert \leq \frac{b-a}{2} \sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^J \frac{n_j^5}{n\, n_{j,0}^2\,n_{j,1}^2}(1+\epsilon_{n_j}')}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\alpha)}{2n}}.

    \textit{Bernstein Interval} To generalize the confidence interval to SCREs, we separately extend Theorem~\ref{thm:neyman-conc} and the underlying \emph{oracle} Bernstein concentration inequality and combine them via a union bound.

    First, we consider $\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^\mathrm{S}$. From the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:neyman-conc}, we know that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda_j\,(\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j]-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j)\right)\right] \leq
        \frac{\lambda_j^2c_j\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j]}{1+c_j\lambda_j},

    for each each $j$ and all $\lambda_j \geq 0$, where $c_j := (b-a)^2c(n_{j,0},n_{j,1})/n_j$. Let $\lambda \in\mathbb{R}$ and set $\lambda_j = s_j^2 \lambda$. Then, we can again use independence between the strata to bound the moment generating function:

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
        \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda (\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^\mathrm{S}]-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^\mathrm{S})\right)\right] = 
        \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\sum_{j=1}^J s_j^2\, (\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j]-\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j)\right)\right]\\
        \leq \exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^J \frac{\lambda^2s_j^2\,c_js_j^2 \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j]}{1+\lambda c_j s_j^2}\right)
        \leq \exp\Bigg(\frac{\lambda^2 c^*}{1+\lambda c^*} \underbrace{\sum_{j=1}^J s_j^2 \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j]}_{\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^\mathrm{S}]}\Bigg),
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        {\end{multline*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[1]\relax}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    where $c^* := \max_{j} c_j s_j^2$. Tracing the remainder of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:neyman-conc} with $c$ replaced by $c^*$, we obtain that
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    \label{eq:neyman-scre}
        \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^\mathrm{S}]^{1/2} \leq (\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^\mathrm{S})^{1/2} + (b-a) \max_{j} \left\{\frac{n_j}{n}\sqrt{c(n_{j,0},n_{j,1})}\right\} \sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}},
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

        {

    with probability $1-\delta$ for any $\delta \in (0,1)$.

    Second, we turn to the oracle Bernstein concentration inequality. Due to Theorem~\ref{thm:rina-bernstein}, we know that

        \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda_j (\hat{\tau}^j_{n_j}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}^j_{n_j}])\right)\right] \leq 
        \exp\left(\frac{\lambda^2_j (1+\epsilon_{n_j}') \Big(\frac{n_{j,0}\,n_{j,1}}{n_j^2}+4\epsilon_{n_j}'\Big) \frac{n_j-1}{n_{j,0}\,n_{j,1}} \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j]}{2\Big(1-\frac{\lvert \lambda_j\rvert}{3} \frac{n_j-1}{n_{j,0}\,n_{j,1}} (b-a)(1+\epsilon_{n_j}')
        \Big)}\right)

    Let $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$ such that

        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
    set $\lambda_j = \lambda s_j$ and recall the definiton of $\epsilon_{n_j}'' := 4\epsilon_{n_j}'\frac{n_j(n_j-1)}{n_{j,0}\,n_{j,1}}-\frac{1}{n_j}$. We again use independence between strata to combine the moment generating functions of the individual strata and then find an upper bound:

        \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda (\hat{\tau}_n-\tau_n)\right)\right] &= \mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda \sum_{j=1}^J s_j(\hat{\tau}_{n_j}^j-\tau_n)\right)\right]\\
        &\leq \exp\left(\lambda^2\sum_{j=1}^J \frac{s_j^2(1+\epsilon_{n_j}') \frac{1+\epsilon_{n_j}''}{n_j} \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^j]}{2\Big(1-\frac{\lvert \lambda\rvert s_j}{3} \frac{n_j-1}{n_{j,0}\,n_{j,1}} (b-a)(1+\epsilon_{n_j}')
        \Big)}\right)\\
        &\leq \exp\left(\frac{\lambda^2 \max_{j}\left\{(1+\epsilon_{n_j}')(1+\epsilon_{n_j}'')/n_j\right\} \mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{\mathrm{ney}}^\mathrm{S}]}{2\Big(1-\frac{\lvert \lambda \rvert}{3}(b-a) \max_{j} \left\{(1+\epsilon_{n_j}')(n_j-1)/(n_{j,0}\,n_{j,1})\right\}\Big)}\right)

    Inverting this bound similarly to Theorem~\ref{thm:rina-bernstein}, we obtain that with probability $1-2\delta$,

        +\frac{b-a}{n}\log(3/\alpha) \Bigg[\frac{1}{3} \max_j \left\{\frac{(1+\epsilon_{n_j}')n(n_j-1)}{n_{j,0}\,n_{j,1}}\right\}\\
        + \sqrt{2\max_j\left\{\frac{(1+\epsilon_{n_j}')(1+\epsilon_{n_j}'')\,n}{n_j}\right\}} \,\,\max_{j} \left\{\frac{n_j}{n}\sqrt{c(n_{j,0},n_{j,1})}\right\}\Bigg],

<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:perm-avg}
    For any matrix $B \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ and arbitrary permutation matrices $\Pi \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$,
     
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}}
